@@2664893 <p> Floods ( again ) in southern Manitoba . Ferocious forest fires ( again ) in the Northwest Territories . Memories still fresh from last year 's terrible floods in Calgary . Summer in Canada . <p> Canada 's climate is changing , and with that change goes more extreme weather conditions . We are not immune from global warming caused by man-made greenhouse gas emissions ; we just have to adapt differently from other places . <p> Weather is , of course , day-to-day , month-to-month stuff . Climate change is certainly not responsible for the fact that it 's raining today or that this month has been wetter or drier than usual . Weather is short term ; climate change is long term . But the two are linked . And even if we could stop all additional greenhouse gases from entering the atmosphere tomorrow , the climate would still change , because those gases ( notably carbon and methane ) stay trapped for a very long time in the atmosphere . <p> So countries have to adapt , or try to adapt , something that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ n't quite know which impact will hit where and when . It 's easier , but often more costly , to react than anticipate . <p> In 2008 , many of Canada 's leading climate scientists and scientists from other disciplines produced a voluminous report on adaptation to climate change . Predictably , the Harper government buried its release on a Friday afternoon . So , too , the government tried to bury the recent update , putting the report on the Natural Resources Canada website without any fanfare . <p> But it 's there : an enormous compendium of information from Canadian experts who report , among other conclusions , that " extreme weather events are a key concern for Canada , and there is growing confidence that some types of extreme events will increase in frequency and/or intensity as the climate continues to warm . " <p> Over the last six decades , Canada 's average temperatures have risen 1.5 degrees Celsius , with warming happening much faster in northern Canada . The frequency of cold nights has dropped ; the frequency of warm days has increased . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ sea ice is declining ( as everyone knows ) in the Arctic but also along parts of the Atlantic coast . <p> Western Canadian glaciers , headwaters of some important rivers , " are losing mass and shrinking rapidly to the smallest extents in several millennia . " Glaciers in British Columbia and Alberta have lost respectively 11 and 25 per cent of their surface areas -- very alarming long-term trends , especially for Alberta , which is a largely dry province that depends on rivers flowing from the Rockies to water agricultural production and grasslands . <p> The experts predicted that " further changes are inevitable . On average , warmer temperatures and more rainfall are expected for the country as a whole , with increases in extreme heat and heavy rainfall events , and declines in snow and ice cover . " They predict " more frequent heavy precipitation events , with an associated increased risk of flooding . " <p> Ice cover duration for lakes in general is expected to decrease by a month by mid-century , and " a nearly ice-free summer is considered a strong possibility @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . " <p> Changes tied to climate can sometimes be positive . An ice-free Arctic opens up economic opportunities . Perhaps warmer temperatures will extend crop production , although they might shorten the ski industry 's chance to earn money . Golf , a sport in decline , might benefit from a longer season , as would warm-weather tourism resorts . <p> On balance , however the changes are costly . No one knows this better than the insurance industry whose leaders have been in the vanguard of talking about climate change and the need to take measures both to reduce emissions and prepare for more adaption . After all , the industry paid out an estimated $1.7-billion following the southern Alberta floods last year . <p> Across central B.C. , communities face a grim future in the not-too-distant future because the mountain pine beetle has ravaged forests . The only antidote to the beetle is a very cold winter , and these have been rare in recent years . The beetle is taking away the future livelihood of B.C. lumber towns and has winged its way across the Rockies and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it 's at least arguable that the double whammy threat to forestry and agriculture makes Alberta the most vulnerable province to the impacts of global warming .

@@2666348 <h> Op-Ed : When will the pilot whale hunt end in the Faroe Islands ? Special <p> The pilot whale hunt in the Faroe Islands will end in the not too distant future . In this the Faroese are mostly in agreement , even many of those who are pro-whaling . But until that happens , whale advocates will need to be patient . <p> ( Part one of a three-part series ) <p> " It wo n't bother me when it ends . " His hands relaxed on the steering wheel , David* is on his way from the airport in Vagar to T ? rshavn , the capital of the Faroe Islands . It 's a drive through a spectacular , fjord-carved landscape . The 25-year-old taxi driver is speaking of the grindadr ? p ( also called ' grind ' ) , the traditional Faroese pilot whale hunt . <p> If there 's a catch around here and I have no whale meat in the freezer , I will take part . <p> David 's attitude is representative of many Faroese . Surveys in recent @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ still behind the whaling which is vehemently criticised internationally . But at the same time most of the younger and middle-aged locals would shed no tears at the end of the hunts . <p> I hear this opportunistic attitude repeatedly during my stays in the Atlantic islands at 62 degrees north . The pilot whale hunting is an exciting , bloody spectacle that brings some diversion to many residents , in perhaps the most remote corner of Europe -- and considerable quantities of free whale meat , as well as international attention . <p> An adrenaline rush related to the hunting instinct of the men of a tiny , close-knit island nation which survived for centuries on little other than fish , sheep , birds -- and whale meat . <p> Stunning beauty . The Faroes are perfect for ecotourism , with breathtaking landscapes and -- yes -- a potential to see whales and dolphins at sea , among birds and other marine wildlife . <p> " The grindadr ? p will stop on its own " added David after a while , as he entered a long , photo controlled @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . For that , activists who want to tell us what to do are not needed . On the contrary . " This too , is how many Faroese see the issue . <p> The road connects the island of Vagar to the main island of Streymoy by a spectacular tunnel under a mile-wide stretch of water . David laughs , Whaling for the youngsters ? They 're not that interested in whale hunts . If they have a good computer game , you can no longer get them out of the door . <p> Innovation , enterprising people and the latest technology have brought the semi-autonomous island republic , which is under Danish administration , a very modern standard of living . So the grindadr ? p may well seem like a bizarre anachronism , if you do not take Faroese history into account . <p> And the pilot whale hunt is no longer really ' traditional ' . Up until less than 100 years ago , men in rowing boats drove marine mammals into the killing bays using pure muscle power . @@2668132 <h> Is $100 oil here to stay ? <p> " The world of energy may have changed forever , " according to Professor James Hamilton of the University of California . " Hundred dollar oil is here to stay . " <p> Hamilton , who is one of the most respected economists writing about oil , made his bold prediction in a paper on The Changing Face of World Oil Markets , published on July 20 . <p> " Old hands in the oil patch may view recent developments as a continuation of the same old story , wondering if the high prices of the last decade will prove another transient cycle with which technological advances will eventually catch up , " he wrote . " But there have been dramatic changes over the last decade that could mark a major turning point . " <p> The shale revolution will turn out to be only a pause in the upward trend in prices , Hamilton argues , as growing demand from emerging economies and stagnant supplies from conventional oil fields push prices higher in the long term . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to historical lows , it seems more accurate to view the emerging tight oil plays as a factor that can mitigate for a while what would otherwise be the tendency for prices to continue to rise . " <p> IGNORING SHALE The problem with Hamilton 's analysis is that it largely ignores the impact of the shale revolution on the economics of oil production and understates the tremendous variability in real oil prices in response to changes in technology . <p> The professor devotes just 400 words out of almost 4,000 to discussing the production of crude oil and gas from shale formations . <p> Most of that discussion focuses on the high cost of drilling and fracturing shale wells ; the rapid decline in production ; the alleged unprofitability of shale wells ; and question of whether the conditions that produced the shale revolution in North American can be replicated in other parts of the world . <p> But this part of the paper is also the weakest , and it highlights the fundamental limitations with Hamilton 's entire argument about the increasing difficulty and costs of producing crude oil @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in oil and gas production from shale formations in North America , and the abundance of shale resources around the world , has discredited theories about peaking oil production . <p> The simple theory that supplies will run out has been reframed as a more sophisticated one about rising prices . <p> Peak oil supporters now point to the increasing cost of oil production , diminishing energy return on investment and the diminishing energy return on energy invested to claim that it is becoming harder and more expensive to sustain , let alone increase , crude output . <p> Prices must continue to rise in real terms , they say , to reflect the increasing cost of producing crude and to restrain demand . Price increases will prove to be just as disruptive as physically running out of the stuff . <p> Hamilton 's paper lends influential support to this view . He notes that oil demand is now being driven by rising incomes in emerging markets , even as high prices restrain consumption in the advanced economies . <p> He also claims that much of the growth in oil production @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " lower quality " natural gas liquids , which have a much lower energy content and energy density than conventional crude . <p> Production of conventional crude oil has stagnated , despite surging prices and unprecedented spending on exploration and production activity . <p> " Depletion of older reservoirs and the high cost of developing new resources " explain why conventional oil output has not responded to climbing prices , Hamilton concludes . <p> SHALE SKEPTIC The paper is skeptical about whether shale oil can alter the supply outlook fundamentally because of its high production costs and the difficulty of replicating the boom outside the United States . But this is the least convincing part of Hamilton 's argument . <p> The paper confuses the struggling economics of dry gas wells with the much more attractive economics of wet gas and oil plays . <p> If oil wells were not extremely profitable , North Dakota and Texas would not be experiencing a drilling boom , with demand for both rigs and petroleum engineers at the highest level for three decades . <p> In focusing on decline rates , Hamilton ignores the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , which in many cases is higher than from conventional wells . <p> The second section of the paper suggests that much of the increase in oil output since 2005 has in fact been " low quality " natural gas liquids rather than true crude , but then the fifth section acknowledges production from shale has increased U.S. crude output by a net 2.3 million barrels per day . <p> In fact , statistics from the U.S. Energy Information Administration show the shale boom has produced a dramatic increase in both natural gas liquids and true crudes . It is simply not true to imply that the oil industry is finding only " low quality " hydrocarbons . <p> One of the biggest problems of the paper is that it confuses the period between 2005 and 2008 , when output was struggling to meet demand , with the more recent period from 2009 through 2014 , when output from shale has grown quite quickly and global oil demand growth has slowed . <p> PRICE FORECAST Even if shale continues to boost overall U.S. oil production , " it is abundantly clear @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ values of a decade ago " , Hamilton argues . <p> According to the BP Statistical Review of World Energy , the real price of crude oil , adjusting for inflation , has ranged from as much as $120 per barrel to as little as $10 since 1861 . <p> The price is currently close to the top of its historical range , while in the 1990s and early 2000s it was near the bottom . <p> So oil prices could retreat from their current highs by $20 , $30 or even $40 per barrel and still remain quite high in historical terms . <p> By comparing current exceptionally high prices with the very low ones that prevailed " a decade ago " , Hamilton risks using a misleading baseline . <p> North American shale is currently the marginal source of supply in the world oil market , and most producers claim they can break even at $70 or even $60 per barrel . <p> TECHNOLOGY Prices have varied enormously over the 155-year history of the oil industry , mostly in response to large discoveries and changes in technology . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 1930s was largely due to the discovery and development of massive new fields in East Texas . <p> Low prices from the 1940s through the 1960s owed much to the massive discoveries in the Middle East around the Persian Gulf and in North Africa . <p> Massive new fields in Siberia and Alaska , as well as the development of deep offshore drilling in the North Sea , eventually contributed to price declines in the late 1980s and through the 1990s . But the history of oil prices is as much about the history of technology as field discoveries . <p> In a very basic sense , production costs have been rising since the industry was born . As in all extractive industries , the easiest oil was developed first , and more expensive oil has been developed later . Hamilton 's argument that costs are increasing could have been made at every stage in the oil industry 's history . <p> The first wells in Pennsylvania tapped oil buried less than 100 feet below the surface . By the mid years of the 20th century , wells were being bored @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the industry turned to offshore drilling , which was even more tricky and expensive . Now it is mastering the art of drilling horizontally rather than vertically . <p> But productivity has also increased as companies have learned to target the highest yielding formations and drill faster and more accurately . Hamilton 's paper is silent on all these matters , and that is ultimately what makes it unconvincing . <p> The paper is also silent about climate change , policies to restrict the consumption of fossil fuels , and the growing challenge to oil-based fuels from natural gas even in the transport market , all of which could depress real prices in the medium to long run . <p> So while Hamilton concludes that $100 oil is here to stay , in real terms , the outlook is far less certain . In fact , a betting man , looking at the price history , might conclude prices are currently abnormally high and due for a fall . 
@@2670469 <p> Tuesday , July 29 , 2014 , 4:03 PM - There 's a massive forest in the centre of Alaska -- and NASA technology has captured it with incredible detail . <p> " The U.S. Forest Service Inventory and Analysis program -- the largest network of forest inventory plots in the world -- does not include 450,000 square kilometers of forest in the interior of Alaska simply because it is so difficult to reach , " says Doug Morton , a scientist based at NASA 's Goddard Space Flight Center in Maryland , in a statement . <p> That -- according to NASA -- is an area larger than the entire state of California , which is about 404,000 square kilometers . <p> Now , researchers can get up close and personal with this relatively unexplored expanse of land in Alaska . NASA has recently released the detailed images these so-called ' hidden ' forests , as seen on July 10 , 2014 . <p> The images were captured during a six-week mission which will wrap up in August . <p> Researchers from NASA and the U.S. @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ low altitude in a small airplane equipped with a portable imaging system that can capture high-res photographs . <p> When the mission wraps up , scientists will be using the images to create a more detailed map of Alaska 's forests . <p> NASA says that by flying just 335 metres above the ground , they 're able to capture images that space satellites ca n't see . <p> " We are getting photographs at ten centimeter resolution and science data ... at one meter resolution , " said Morton . <p> The team says the images will help scientists track how Alaska 's forests are adapting to climate change and wildfire activity . <p> " As the planet warms , we may see generalized greening in low-resolution satellite imagery , but what 's driving the greening ? Are mosses replacing forests as wetlands dry out , or are woody plants increasing in abundance ? These are the kinds of questions we can answer with a combination of satellite data and high-res aerial imagery , " Morton said . 
@@2670530 <h> ENDANGERED : American eel numbers in the St. Lawrence River are plummeting <p> St. Lawrence River Institute scientist Matt Windle gets up close and personal with an American eel . <p> CORNWALL , Ontario - They may look snake-like and come complete with a revoltingly slimy exterior - but biology experts are working to save the American eel . <p> The eel was once a staple part of the St. Lawrence River ecosystem , but experts are now worried the animal could be in danger of seeing its numbers plummet beyond the staggering statistics suggested to date . <p> " Now it 's rare to meet someone that has seen a wild eel in Ontario , and the public is losing the important connection that First Nations and European settlers once had with this species , " said St. Lawrence River Institute research scientist Matt Windle . " Eel populations in the Great Lakes system declined severely in the 1990s , likely as a cumulative result of habitat loss , overfishing , and climate change . " <p> Experts like Windle are expected to meet next month @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a science symposium . <p> In the meantime biologists are still trying to get their heads around the drop in eel numbers . <p> Because of steeply declining populations , American eels are classified as endangered in Ontario . The U.S. Fish and Wildlife is considering American eels for listing under the U.S. Endangered Species Act . <p> The average number of eels migrating up the St. Lawrence River near Cornwall decreased to roughly 40,000 per year in 2013 from one milion in the 1980s . <p> Windle said American eels used to be kings of the St. Lawrence River , helping to shape the habitat stretching well west of Cornwall . <p> " Historically , eels would have had a stabilizing effect on Great Lakes ecosystems , through their super-abundance , long life spans , and role at the top of the food chain , " he said . <p> The American eel spawns in seawater but spends most of its life in freshwater lakes and streams . Its spawning zone is the Sargasso Sea east of the Bahamas and southwest of Bermuda in the North Atlantic Ocean at reported @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ lays up to 20 million eggs during her lifetime . When they hatch , the transparent leaf-like larval eels spread to great distances , moving with the ocean currents . They assume the shape of the adults as they reach the mouths of freshwater streams after about a year at sea . <p> Females found in the Great Lakes watershed can remain in freshwater for upwards of 20 years , growing to more than 40 inches and seven pounds before returning to the Sargasso Sea to spawn and die . <p> Males are considerably smaller and seldom exceed 24 inches in length . <p> Only female American eels are found in the Great Lakes and St. Lawrence River . It 's a mystery where the males go because they are present in numbers equal to females at the mouth of the St. Lawrence . <p> American eels have been found far inland as the Grand River near Lake Michigan , and in Ashland County on Lake Superior in Wisconsin . <p> Eel flesh is considered a delicacy in many parts of the world , especially among Europeans and the Japanese . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and other eels . Although commercial fishing of American eels is now banned in the Great Lakes states and Ontario ; a limited fishery remains in Quebec . <p> Dams also undermine the species , thwarting migration upstream in the St. Lawrence River . Hydroelectric facility operators are making adjustments to allow passage . Ontario Hydro and the New York Power Authority have installed eel laddersthat have the potential to restore healthy populations . <p> The river instituteis currently studying the habitat usage of eels in the St. Lawrence River as part of a research project funded by the Ontario Ministry of Natural Resources in collaboration with Ontario Power Generation , the Mohawk Council of Akwesasne , and the Department of Fisheries and Oceans . <p> The goal of the project is to fill in critical knowledge gaps regarding the preferred habitat conditions of eels at various life stages . Eels are considered habitat generalists and have the widest habitat range of any freshwater species in North America . <h> Comments <p> Comments <p> Your name*Email*Comment* <h> Recent comments <p> Linda Heron <p> August 03 , 2014 - 06:09 <p> Unfortunately @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lawrence River had in the decline of the American Eel populations was not even mentioned here . In the past , little attempt has been made to mitigate these effects in Ontario , despite the fact that hydroelectric facilities have been inducing ongoing harm for more than 100 years . Even today , a basic mitigation technique such as installation of fish ladders is not a requirement in the construction of new hydroelectric dams in the province . The environmental and ecological damage due to hydroelectric impacts are well known throughout the world . One of the most famous cases in North America involves the devastating cumulative impacts of hydropower on Pacific Salmon stocks in the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Ontario where dams are considered to be a major factor in the extirpation of Ontario 's Atlantic Salmon stock , one of the important causes of significant anthropogenic mortalities and decline of Ontario 's American Eel , and a key threat to Ontario 's declining Lake Sturgeon populations . It 's also the dirtiest of the fossil fuels , an inconvenient truth that does n't appear to bother Mr. Abbott . Australia is the world 's fourth largest coal producer , trailing only China , the United States and India . <p> Australia recently took steps to make its fledgling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ designed to remove the shackles from its badly slumping coal industry . Australia , like Canada , would have a lot of work to do under any agreement , given their respective places among the world 's leading per-capita carbon emitters . <p> Much like the business case for Canadian bitumen , the appeal of Australia 's vast coal reserves lies in the country 's close proximity to a huge market . Chinese electricity demand is to Australian coal miners what the American motorist is to Canadian oil producers . In both cases , the appetite for what 's on offer will soon by waning . Prior to the recession in 2008 , thermal coal prices peaked at $190 a tonne . At today 's prices , Australia 's coal mines , as well as those around the world , are losing money . <p> The largest U.S. coal producer , for one , lost more than $500-million last year due to falling prices . Since 2011 , Peabody Energy Corp . has watched nearly 80 per cent of its market capitalization go up in smoke . In May , Standard &amp; Poor 's downgraded its rating to BB-minus , three notches below investment grade . <p> The outlook for coal does n't look much better to investors than it does to credit ratings agencies . With coal prices suffering and losses mounting , more divestitures in the sector are anticipated . According to S&amp;P , coal is poised to see a shrinking share of the world 's power generation market , which could lead to some coal reserves becoming stranded assets . <p> Investors in Canadian oil stocks would do well to take a long look at the parallels between the oil sands and Australia 's coal industry . In Canada , Prime Minister Harper 's oil-based ambitions could well have the same fate in store . <p> Jeff Rubin is the former chief economist of CIBC World Markets and the author of the award-winning Why Your World Is About To Get A Whole Lot Smaller . @@2673254 <p> Benjamin Franklin is credited with the phrase , " An ounce of prevention is worth a pound of cure " . This statement has survived over two hundred years because it makes sense , common sense . But then up pops the Canada Revenue Agency like Monty Python 's Spanish Inquisition to inform us all that preventing poverty can not be the aim of Oxfam Canada , one of the most trusted and respected aid agencies in the world . <p> What kind of Orwellian doublespeak is this ? According to Canada 's charity overseers it 's OK to help alleviate poverty but for Oxfam to maintain its not-for-profit status no longer can it aim to " prevent " poverty . This story would have fit so well into Robin Whitaker 's recent Gadfly column " The Trouble with Charity " . <p> To declare the prevention of poverty invalid as a charitable act is farcical , most worthy of a Rick Mercer Rant or a 22 Minutes skit . But it does have serious ramifications . <h> Alleviation Vs AND Prevention <p> How does one distinguish @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ instance , are designed to alleviate hunger . They do nothing to prevent it . According to Revenue Canada food banks provide a valid charitable service . However , anyone lobbying for lower drug costs , increased financial supports or advocating for affordable housing are not worthy of charitable status . Yet even the provincial government has recognized that the best way to reduce poverty is via a Poverty Reduction Strategy that focuses on supports to both alleviate and prevent poverty . It is a development truism that to eradicate poverty one needs to focus on its causes . <p> The same is true globally . Many millions find themselves desperately poor due to a particular natural emergency such as an earthquake ( e.g. Haiti ) or a typhoon ( e.g. The Philippines ) . Other explanations why so many people find themselves in poverty are more political in nature -- for example , the Gaza bombardment or the refugee exodus of Syria . <p> Oxfam has a strong record in responding to such disasters in providing safe water and sanitation to tens of thousands of people , women , men @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 25 emergency crises at a time worldwide . It is from direct experience of responding to and assisting desperately poor people that Oxfam feels duty-bound to raise its voice and to offer solutions that will help prevent future crises . <p> Currently Oxfam staff who live and work in Gaza are distributing emergency food vouchers and providing safe water to nearly 50,000 people who have fled their homes and who are sheltering in schools , churches , mosques and hospitals . Oxfam also operates a mobile healthcare clinic in Gaza . This day to day experience in Gaza has compelled Oxfam to call for a lasting peace and an end to the Israeli blockade : " The international community needs to redouble efforts to achieve a just and lasting peace that brings security and development to all Palestinians and Israelis . Israel must end the blockade on Gaza , which is collectively punishing an entire civilian population . " <h> Overcoming poverty " an act of justice " : Mandela <p> For over 30 years I worked as an educator and campaigner for Oxfam Canada . It is man-made and it can be overcome and eradicated by the actions of human beings . " <p> For Mandela poverty was a social crime and as such we needed to speak out , stand up and take action against this very injustice . Such thinking can also be found in the writing of Paulo Friere , a Brazilian educator who worked closely with the poor of his country . Friere saw a distinction between false and true generosity . <p> " True generosity consists precisely in fighting to destroy the causes which nourish false charity . Oxfam is a rights-based organization that is committed to the eradication of poverty . While I no longer work for Oxfam I feel confident in saying that Oxfam will continue its humanitarian work of aiding people who find themselves in all forms of distress . Oxfam will continue to support partners who are fighting to have their human rights recognized and enforced ; women owning land , children attending school , workers treated fairly are all part of this mix . <p> Oxfam will also continue to join with others in demanding that world leaders own up to their obligations on such issues as aid , climate change and respect for human rights . And Oxfam will continue to campaign and advocate on issues such as the Robin Hood Tax , a small financial tax , as a source of funds that could go along to helping alleviate and prevent poverty . <p> As for the Canada Revenue Agency , it will continue to administer the tax laws for Canada @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ up with the thinking of Benjamin Franklin and accept that an ounce of prevention is worth more than a pound of cure . <p> Bill Hynd worked for Oxfam Canada as an educator and campaigner for 30 years . @@2675866 <h> Separating fact from fiction in the claim that oil and gas companies are over-subsidized <p> Is the energy industry a giver or a taker ? <p> By Anthony A. Davis <p> July 31 , 2014 <p> Illustration Steve McPhee <p> When asked if the Canadian petroleum industry receives government subsidies , Bob Bleaney , a vice-president with the Canadian Association of Petroleum Producers ( CAPP ) , starts off with a fudgy : " I would have to qualify this answer . " <p> Bringing up the question of subsidies is like touching a bruise . Although the subject has long been a source of ache for the industry , the pain may be shrinking somewhat as the federal government has demonstrably cut down various supports for the sector . For instance , Ottawa announced with the tabling of its 2012 budget that the petroleum sector would , after 2015 , no longer be eligible to claim the Atlantic Investment Tax Credit . The AITC saves the sector about $85 million in taxes each year . <p> The AITC may be phased out for oil and gas @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ industry remain . Especially as the definition of a subsidy is as stretchy as Elastigirl from The Incredibles . <p> Subsidies are a critical part of many countries ' policies . They can nurture emerging industries and give them toeholds in already established competitive markets . No doubt , not long after the Dingman No. 1 well produced a gusher in Turner Valley a century ago , federal and provincial subsidies helped foster the toddling Canadian industry into the giant it now is . An industry , CAPP reminds , that creates tens of thousands of jobs and currently generates about $18 billion annually in royalties , taxes and other payments to Canadian governments . <p> But , depending on who gets them , subsidies can also squeeze out other industries . As the International Monetary Fund ( IMF ) put in a report on subsidy reform last January , " While aimed at protecting consumers , subsidies aggravate fiscal imbalances , crowd out priority public spending , and depress private investment , including in the energy sector . Subsidies also distort resource allocation by encouraging excessive energy consumption , artificially @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ energy , and accelerating the depletion of natural resources . " <p> " The amount of tax revenue from the oil and gas sector that the federal government is foregoing may already exceed the amount it is collecting -- $1.3 billion in 2012 . " <p> And so , continues Bleaney , CAPP 's vice-president for Ottawa and Eastern/Atlantic Canada , if he were to say the oil and gas industry did not receive subsidies in any way shape or form , somebody would come back and point to something that could conceivably be construed as one . " That 's why we are being thoughtful to not be expressively definitive in that statement at this point . " <p> What Bleaney does want to say specifically is that , in CAPP 's view , the Canadian oil and gas industry is treated no differently than any other industry by government . It operates , essentially , under the same fiscal regime as mining , manufacturing , forestry and other industries . <p> In an op-ed last May for The Hill Times , an independent political weekly , Bleaney wrote that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ identical to the rate for other companies and that capital expenditures and expenses are deducted using the same principles and comparable rates to other industries . " We do n't accept that we are being differentially subsidized , " he says . <p> The critics see things differently and try to specify the value of subsidies given by governments annually in the form of tax breaks , royalty reductions , direct subsidies and other support to the Canadian petroleum industry . That 's not an easy thing for anyone to estimate these days since Statistics Canada stopped compiling numbers on federal subsidy distributions back in 2009 . <p> But the critics ' numbers bounce around all over the place depending on the definition they use . Some consider certain externalities like car accidents as subsidies . They argue that most vehicles are propelled by internal combustion engines running on gasoline and the fuel is indirectly implicated in the crash . By this line of reasoning , they find fault with the industry for not contributing to accident costs . <p> The varying interpretations of the word " subsidy " and the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ average Canadian to figure out just how much of his or her taxes , provincial and federal governments are doling out to an industrial sector that is , by some measures , sturdier and more profitable than any other in the country . <p> " Projects in the natural resources sector have different life cycles than other industries . " <p> " Subsidies are a mess , " says Dave Sawyer , an environmental economist and CEO of EnviroEconomics , a consulting firm specializing in designing sustainable environmental policies . " To start , there are two strong views that define whether there is or is not a subsidy . In the polluter-pay environmental view , explains Sawyer , who has investigated Canadian fossil fuel subsidies for the International Institute for Sustainable Development 's Global Subsidies Initiative , the oil and gas sector is accused of using the environment as a dump for the waste water and greenhouse gas emissions it produces in extraction , refining and final use of its products . For the most part , the environmentalist side argues , the industry does not pay for these externalized @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ a subsidy , and gasoline prices are out of sync with the real societal and environmental costs of each barrel of extracted oil . <p> " Then there is the development view , " explains Sawyer . It parallels the government view that there are no subsidies at all . " There are just programs in response to competitive pressures that try to help the sector compete for investment flow and keep economic activity up . " Taxes and royalties are kept low to keep investment and production levels high . <p> In January the IMF , embracing the environmentalist definition , called for international reform of fossil fuel subsidies and pegged Canadian subsidies supporting the coal , natural gas and oil industries at a whopping $34 billion per year . <p> Contacted by the left-leaning Tyee online news site , IMF researchers helpfully provided a detailed breakdown of those Canadian " subsidies . " The bulk , about $19.4 billion in 2011 , was identified as uncollected taxes and externalized costs such as carbon emissions , pollution , traffic accidents and even road congestion . Sawyer says many of those @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ report . " I think any reasonable people looking at it would dismiss the IMF view as being overly inclusive in its definition . " Because some of those externalities are unpaid for by society in general , it 's unfair to pin them just on the fossil fuel industry , he argues . <p> In a 2010 report prepared by EnviroEconomics for the IISD examining subsidy support in three provinces -- Alberta , Saskatchewan , and Newfoundland and Labrador -- Sawyer used the World Trade Organization 's definition of a subsidy . It 's one agreed to by 153 member countries including Canada . That definition identifies four types of subsidies : where government provides direct transfers of funds or liabilities , foregoes revenue , provides or purchases goods or services , or provides income or price support . EnviroEconomics estimated that provincial and federal governments were providing about $2.8 billion annually in subsidies to the oil sector in the three provinces . The federal government share of that is about $1.4 billion annually . <p> The study identified 63 different subsidy programs , 18 of which were in Alberta @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ exploration and development by reducing the costs of those activities through tax breaks and royalty reductions . But it does n't mention , for instance , the enormous financial commitments the province has made to the Sturgeon Refinery . Those were worth $233 million to industry in 2009 and the program continues today . <p> " Projects in the natural resources sector have different life cycles than other industries , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 25 or 30 year cycles . For the first years , you are not making any money , but you have big costs . And when you are finally making money , it 's only legitimate to take into account the costs that you 've engaged previously . So those tax treatments are not subsidies in themselves . @@2675887 <p> Thursday , July 31 , 2014 , 1:01 PM - An international team of scientists , who are studying the vast deposits of methane trapped on the floor of the Arctic Ocean , have captured their first look at plumes of this powerful greenhouse gas bubbling up through water . This discovery could lead to better forecasting , but it also has serious implications for Earth 's climate in the years to come . <p> Much of the science of global warming has been focused on the study of carbon dioxide ( CO2 ) as the main culprit in the current trend of rising global temperatures and resulting climate disruption we 're seeing today . This is only appropriate , since CO2 is , by far , the largest contributor to the problem - at least by total amount of gas that 's added to the atmosphere on a yearly basis . However , methane ( chemical formula CH4 ) is another greenhouse gas that 's been ' on the radar ' for climate scientists , because it is much better than carbon dioxide at trapping heat @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ time , kg for kg , methane traps over 85 times more heat than carbon dioxide . Over a 100 year period , that ratio drops , but even averaged over that long methane is still over 20 times more potent a greenhouse gas than CO2 . Methane is only a small contributor to the overall climate change we 're seeing now , but there are fears that this could change in the years to come . <p> Credit : Pete Hill , SWERUS-C3 <p> Although there are natural sources of methane in the atmosphere , according to the US EPA , over 60 per cent comes from human activities - industry , agriculture and garbage . Scientists have also discovered large deposits of methane in the Arctic , where it has been locked away in a slushy frozen form on the sea floor called methane hydrates , which has also been called ' fire ice ' due to the fact that it readily burns , even in its frozen form . These hydrates are kept frozen by the extreme low temperature and crushing pressure at the bottom of the ocean @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ time now , these hydrates could ' melt ' and release the methane in gas form . If that were to happen , all that methane bubbling up to the ocean surface and into the atmosphere would likely result in the accelerated rate of climate change we 're seeing now turning into catastrophic abrupt climate change . <p> Since these methane hydrate deposits were discovered , scientists have been doing what they can to monitor and study them , and now a team from SWERUS-C3 - the Swedish -- Russian -- US Arctic Ocean Investigation of **25;27;TOOLONG Interactions program - have actually spotted methane bubbling up through the water , while measuring a concentration of methane dissolved in the water that 's 10-50 times higher than background levels . <p> " This was somewhat of a surprise , " SWERUS-C3 chief scientist ? rjan Gustafsson , from Stockholm University , wrote in his blog . " While there has been much speculation about the vulnerability of regular marine hydrates along the continental slopes of the Arctic rim , very few actual observations of methane releases due to collapsing marine hydrates on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ short , 3-second video below actually shows the bubbles they caught rising through the water : <p> According to Gustafsson , scientists have noted that the ' end ' of the Gulf Stream in the Atlantic Ocean , which is a relatively warm ' tongue ' of water that flows past northern Europe and through the Arctic Ocean towards East Siberia , may have been warming up in recent years . " Our SWERUS-C3 program is hypothesizing that this heating may lead to destabilization of upper portion of the slope methane hydrates , " he wrote . " This may be what we now for the first time are observing . " <p> Does this mean that the disaster scenario is now developing ? Unfortunately , at the moment , that 's an unknown . The SWERUS-C3 team will be continuing to monitor the location as long as the weather holds out for their expedition . However , as the Stockholm University press release stated : " These early glimpses of what may be in store for a warming Arctic Ocean could help scientists project the future releases of the strong @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@2676031 <h> Richmond Hill residents , councillors dispute fate of family land <h> Floodplain policy ' not unusual ' and ' best for town ' <h> Upset landowner <p> By Mike Barrett <p> Larry Thomas is sick with worry over what will happen to the stream on his Richmond Hill property once it is conveyed to the town to protect the neighbourhood from flooding . He and his neighbours are n't happy about town policy that forces homeowners to give up land to the town for stormwater management and some councillors and town staff are worried about a precedent payment to Thomas for the hazardous lands . <h> SIDEBAR <p> WHAT WAS SAID : " We 're not talking about a developer here , we 're talking about a longstanding citizen of the town that 's been here before some of us were even born . He 's maintained this property in excellent condition . " - Vito Spatafora , regional councillor <p> This is one of those stories that , up until this point anyway , has no happy ending . <p> It begins on a pastoral patch of rolling land , dappled streams and towering trees and it ends on the same plot of land -- but the peace is replaced with bitterness , anger and worry for the future . <p> Passersby may have noticed the difference at 41 Walmer : the once lush lawns are weedy now , the rustic bridge in disrepair . <p> Larry Thomas , who owns the property in Richmond Hill 's Ward 5 , guides a visitor on a tour under the tree boughs , but it 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ trepidation , walking with a cane and pain -- physical and emotional . <p> " I never come down here anymore . It 's too depressing , " he says . <p> That 's because the past is gone , and the future is not what he wanted . He has gone head-to-head with the town and he believes he has lost . <p> Many at the Town of Richmond Hill also believe they have lost , but in another way altogether . <p> No one wants to lose this patch of nature -- not Thomas and his neighbours , not the town or Toronto Region Conservation Authority -- but what the land should look like , and who should pay for it , is where the two sides diverge . <p> Thomas hopes to live out his last days here as did his mother , watching over the birds and waterfowl , flowers and rolling lawns that have been in his family since 1940 . <p> When his family first took ownership of the Richvale-area property , there were no roads west of Yongehurst . Thomas ' grandparents travelled a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Thomas spent summers at this " cottage " in Richmond Hill , wandering the wilderness between Carville Road and Maple Sideroad -- now known as Major Mackenzie . <p> They swam and ate fish from the creek that was alive with crayfish , frogs and turtles . <p> " We were out from dawn to dusk , enjoyed the sweet smell of the air , climbed trees , helped my grandmother with her farming chores , " he wrote in his spring appeal to the committee of adjustment . " The whole scenario was a feast for our imagination . " <p> Imagination is called for these days . There 's only 3/4 of an acre left -- the rest has been split off over the years and built up with homes , his grandmother forced to sell much of the land for " next to nothing " because it was labelled undevelopable floodland , he says . <p> Thomas gingerly guides a visitor down to the creek-bed , pointing out the big old willow his grandfather planted , the orchard with still fruit-bearing cherry , plum and apple @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , he shared tea with his mother and watched the wildlife . <p> Now , he says the waterway up and downstream is littered with glass , needles , condoms , builders ' debris , tires , dead animals and excrement . <p> He worries the future holds the same for his stretch of stream . <p> " We took pride in this and now they want to wipe it all away just because I need to make some money at my age . " <p> Thomas has been sick since last October and decided it 's time to do something with the land to pay for his retirement and live out his days here . <p> He approached the town to see about severing the property , to sell two 50-foot lots and keep the remainder , including the low lands and stream , to rebuild his aging bungalow . <p> The committee of adjustment gave him the thumbs up , but the town 's planning department disagreed and called on council to appeal the committee 's decision before the Ontario Municipal Board . <p> " It 's like the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ out , let 's grab his land now ' , " Thomas says . <p> That is not at all the case , says planning commissioner Ana Bassios . <p> Town staff appealed the decision because the low-lying area is delineated regulatory flood line , hazardous lands that should be conveyed to the municipality in keeping with the town 's Official Plan to protect the area from flooding . <p> Development brings more impermeable surfaces such as roads , sidewalks and parking lots , she said , and climate change is bringing stormier weather . <p> Richmond Hill , like communities everywhere , is experiencing escalating problems with storm water runoff , back yard flooding and erosion of waterways . <p> Environmental restoration , particularly of streams , is essential to manage water flow , or there will be flooding from more frequent storms , according to Bassios . <p> When development comes forward for approval , the town uses the opportunity to add naturalized areas that will enhance the water management system , in this case , about 35 to 40 percent of the Walmer Road lot . <p> But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ his property by $400,000 . <p> " It just makes me so upset . I do n't know how many years I have left , I 'm not in great shape . It 's not part of a greenway or park , it was never flooded . This is insanity . " <p> The only time his land flooded , he said , it was caused by loosened debris washing down from an upstream neighbour 's illegal pond . All around his property the waterway that is now publicly owned for naturalization purposes resembles a " dump " , in contrast to his own mown lawns and park-like setting . <p> At the very least , he 'd like to see a bench , a plaque , public accessibility , and a say on what kind of plants are planted . <p> " They 're not taking care of what 's there -- how can they say they 're going to do a better job here ? " <p> Bruno Artenosi , Thomas ' real estate broker , suggested the town should , at a minimum , waive the park fees that are levied whenever land moves towards development , in this case $13,000 per severed lot . <p> After some debate , council voted to pay Thomas $26,000 . <p> It was the right thing to do , said Thomas ' ward councillor , Nick Papa . <p> " I do n't see anything wrong with this . We 're looking after this gentleman and his inheritance , " he told council . " I do n't see why the heck we 're making such a big deal out of this . $26,000 is peanuts . " <p> Others , however , worried about setting a precedent . <p> The payment amounts to $117,000 per acre , Bassios told council . <p> " If you were to apply this figure to environmental lands on David Dunlap Observatory , we 'd be talking about $11 million . <p> " The precedent will be one that will be known @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ continuous stream where people already have conveyed the hazard lands and ... on every environmental piece of land , every water course . " <p> Ward 4 Councillor David West , who sat on the committee of adjustment from 2010 to 2013 , shared that concern . <p> " On the surface , giving up hazardous lands seems a bit unfair , but that land could never be developed and almost every municipality does it the same way , it 's not like we 're doing anything unusual . " <p> His three years on the committee saw many similar situations where land was conveyed from landowner to the town with no payment . <p> " This will be a huge hit to the town 's revenue and enormous cost to the taxpayer . <p> " I feel for Mr. Thomas ... but there was a huge amount of work with the public developing the strategic plan and official plan that 's supposed to guide us in Richmond Hill and if we start making exceptions , we will end up with a mess . At the end of the day , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's best for the town . " <p> While the town planning department is disappointed with council 's decision to pay Thomas $26,000 , Thomas is also disappointed with the minimal compensation and the prospect of losing his family legacy . <p> At least he has his childhood memories and comfort in knowing that his mother was able to live out her days here , he says .

@@2676195 <p> Smoke rises from stacks as processing slowly resumes at the Imperial Oil refinery in Nanticoke in 2007 . The IMF is calling on Canada to impose raise carbon taxes , but reduce taxation on people . ( J.P . MOCZULSKI/REUTERS ) <p> Smoke rises from stacks as processing slowly resumes at the Imperial Oil refinery in Nanticoke in 2007 . The IMF is calling on Canada to impose raise carbon taxes , but reduce taxation on people . ( J.P . MOCZULSKI/REUTERS ) <p> The International Monetary Fund says Canada and other countries can improve their economies and environment by hiking energy taxes -- while cutting them on people and capital . <p> In a new book , Getting Energy Prices Right : From Principle to Practice , the IMF essentially endorses policies at times advocated by the federal Liberals and NDP calling for what some have termed a " green shift " in the taxation system . The Conservative government , however , has rejected carbon taxes . <p> The premise of the book is that while carbon-based energy was indispensable to economic growth over the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> The solution , the IMF says , is to tax energy to such a level that the revenue pays for energy 's environment and health costs . <p> In a novel and rather ambitious approach , the Washington-based financial institution attempts to calculate the cost of carbon energy -- coal , gas , motor diesel and natural gas -- for 156 countries and proposes precise taxation levels for each country to implement . <p> " The results confirm that many countries -- advanced , emerging , and developing -- are only at base camp with regard to getting energy prices right , " IMF managing director Christine Lagarde says in a foreword to the 199-page book . <p> For Canada , getting the price right could be a shock to the system of consumers and industry . <p> For instance , the IMF says gasoline should be taxed at about $0.55 ( U.S. ) a litre instead of the current 36 cents , and road diesel at about $0.64 per litre , instead of the current 42 cents . <p> The book uses U.S. currency calculations so exact numbers in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to a 52-per-cent increase in the taxes applied to both gasoline and diesel . <p> Meanwhile , the IMF says there should be a $4.90 per gigajoule tax on coal , where there is none now , and natural gas should be taxed at $2.20 per gigajoule , in place of the small subsidy that currently exists . A gigajoule is a unit of energy . <p> In an interview , co-author Ian Parry agreed such proposals have caused a voter backlash whenever suggested , but adds that the trick is to make clear to voters that other taxes , particularly those on income , will be cut by identical amounts . <p> " We are not talking about increasing the overall tax burden ; we are talking about a smarter more efficient way to use taxation to meet a country 's fiscal objectives , " he said . <p> Parry admitted , however , that governments have n't been very successful at communicating the " revenue neutral " message and that voters have been skeptical . One way of trying to convince people they are not being gouged is for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ charges . <p> As well , he said , carbon taxes should be phased in slowly to lessen shocks to the economy . <p> The IMF calculates phasing in the carbon taxes to the levels it suggests would increase Canada 's gross domestic product by 1.4 per cent , reduce carbon emissions by 15 per cent and diminish deaths from air pollution by 25 per cent , the latter mostly from reducing coal use . <p> It cautions that the numbers are estimates only , based on assumptions that can be debated , but that the overall message is valid . <p> " Underpinning the policy recommendations is the notion that taxation can influence behaviour . In much the same way that taxes on cigarettes discourage their overuse , appropriate taxes can discourage overuse of environmentally harmful energy products , " the authors write . <p> On costs , the authors attempt to tally up the impacts of carbon not only on climate change , but also for more common air pollution that affects health , as well as traffic congestion and accidents . <p> Globally , Canada is near the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ industrialized nations . It taxes gas higher than the U.S. , but generally well below taxation levels applied in Europe and Japan . <h> Restrictions <p> All rights reserved . @@2676253 <h> Watching kites fly a joy for bird lovers <h> Small raptors never seen here before <p> All eyes are on the skies over Wellington Crescent for a sighting of a pair of birds that do n't belong here . <p> Two Mississippi kites -- small , grey raptors -- have been spotted thousands of kilometres from home , thrilling Manitoba birdwatchers who 've been flocking to the posh neighbourhood along the Assiniboine River for a glimpse of them . <p> " It 's the first time it 's ever been seen in Manitoba , so it 's pretty exciting from a provincial perspective , " said Peter Taylor , editor in chief of The Birds of Manitoba . He saw the two raptors Monday on Wellington at Montrose Street . <p> On Wednesday , a crowd of about a dozen birdwatchers , some armed with jumbo camera lenses , gathered at the corner on the lookout for the southern couple . <p> One captured an image of a Mississippi kite catching a dragonfly . <p> " That seems to be a specialty of theirs -- catching large @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It 's a very graceful bird . The silhouette is a little bit like a peregrine falcon , but it does n't have the powerful high-speed flight of a falcon . It 's much more soaring and sailing and swooping after insects . It 's got a slower motion of flight than a peregrine , but it 's very , very graceful , " said Taylor . <p> " It 's not spectacular in terms of colour -- it 's various shades of grey -- but it 's beautiful in its movements . " <p> He could n't say why a Mississippi kite -- whose main range is the southern half of the United States and north to around Missouri -- would be so far from home . <p> " Sometimes , rare birds show up as a result of navigation errors or are blown here by storms . " The pair spotted in Winnipeg might just be explorers who went beyond the edge of their range , Taylor said . <p> " There are scattered records of them showing up right across the northeast and north-central states . They do @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ he said . In 1985 and 1992 , Mississippi kites were spotted in Regina around the legislative building , said Taylor . <p> " To have them appear to leapfrog as far north as Winnipeg is exceptional , " he said . <p> " It may be partly climate change , it may be partly habitat change . " <p> The planting of shelterbelts in the Great Plains and southern U.S. has resulted in new habitat for the birds and their population growth , said Taylor . <p> It 's not the first surprising bird to show up in Manitoba lately . <p> " There has been a flurry of unusual sightings , " Taylor said . <p> In May , another kite was spotted -- the swallow-tailed kite , whose population declined in the early 20th century . It was the first recorded sighting of one in Manitoba in more than a century , said Taylor . <p> The Mississippi kites in Winnipeg are well beyond their range but have found their comfort zone and may be starting a family . <p> " They like nesting in shelterbelts and where @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It 's an ideal situation , with a combination of urban forest and river-bottom forest along Wellington Crescent , " he said . He could n't confirm from the look of the two Mississippi kites if they are mates . <p> " The male and female are very similar , " he said . Judging from their behaviour -- staying around Wellington Crescent and Montrose where they 've been spotted for nearly two weeks -- it appears they could be nesting and setting down northern roots , he said . <p> " We certainly hope to see them return and maybe get established , with time . " <h> History <p> You can comment on most stories on winnipegfreepress.com . You can also agree or disagree with other comments .
